<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Fast or thinking?

```text
Some questions need an answer in two seconds. Others need a model that stops and works it through. ChatGPT can do both, and choosing well saves you time, money and embarrassing mistakes. In this lecture you will learn how ChatGPT's fast and thinking modes differ, what changed in twenty twenty six, and a simple rule for choosing every time.
```

Scene 2 — Why this matters

```text
Why does this choice matter? Because speed and depth really are a trade off. Pick a thinking model for a caption and you wait longer and burn through your allowance for no gain. Pick a fast answer for a pricing decision and you may get something that sounds sensible but skipped a constraint. Think of it like choosing between a quick phone call and a proper meeting. Both are useful. Using the wrong one for the job wastes time or produces a poor decision. So let's learn to choose deliberately.
```

Scene 3 — Where answers come from

```text
First, the mental model. Every answer comes from two places. What the model learned in training, and what is in this conversation right now, your messages, files, custom instructions, memory and the results of tools like web search, data analysis or connected apps. When an answer is wrong or generic, ask first, what was missing from the context? Very often, that is the real fix.
```

Scene 4 — Two behaviours

```text
Since the GPT five generation, ChatGPT combines two behaviours. Instant responses handle everyday questions, drafting, rewriting and summarising. Thinking responses reason for longer before answering, which is better for multi step logic, maths, analysis, coding and planning, but slower and more limited on usage. By default ChatGPT can route between them. On paid plans the model picker lets you choose, including thinking levels, and the most capable Pro models are reserved for higher tier plans.
```

Scene 5 — 2026: a fast-moving line-up

```text
The line up moves quickly. Through twenty twenty six ChatGPT moved through several GPT five point x releases, and in September OpenAI announced its GPT six generation, including GPT six Astra, which it describes as its most intelligent model, and faster, lower cost models called Sol and Luna, rolling out to paid plans across the agent experience, Codex and the API. Names, defaults and limits will keep changing. So learn the pattern, fast, thinking and pro, and always check the picker and OpenAI's help centre.
```

Scene 6 — The decision rule

```text
Here is the rule. Rewriting, summarising, brainstorming, translating and quick questions, use Instant. Analysing a spreadsheet, comparing options, planning a budget or debugging a formula, use Thinking. High stakes analysis where depth matters more than speed, use the highest thinking level or a Pro model if your plan has one. And long, multi step work across apps and files, like building a deck from research, belongs to the agent experience we cover in module five.
```

Scene 7 — Fix the prompt first

```text
Before you upgrade the model, fix the prompt. Most weak answers are missing data, constraints or success criteria. A thinking model cannot invent your sales history. Give it the goal, the data, the constraints like total budget and minimum spend per channel, the success criteria, and ask for a table of workings, a list of assumptions and a sensitivity check, what happens if costs rise twenty percent?
```

Scene 8 — Simple example

```text
Here is a simple pair of examples. Rewrite this email subject line to sound friendlier. That is an Instant job. It returns in seconds, and three options are all you need. Now, which of three courier options is cheapest for forty orders a week, given different weight bands and a surcharge for remote areas? That is a Thinking job. The answer needs a small calculation table, and a fast guess could easily pick the wrong courier. Same person, same morning, two different right choices.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example: a Dubai café

```text
A café owner in Dubai asks what price to charge for a new breakfast set. In Instant mode, with no data, she gets a generic range. She switches to Thinking and provides ingredient costs, current prices, footfall by hour and a target margin. Now she gets a cost table, three price scenarios, a break even number of sets per day and a list of assumptions, including that labour is fixed. She corrects it, because weekends need an extra staff member, and the recommendation changes. The mode helped, but the data and her correction made it useful. Notice what changed and what did not. The model mode changed the depth of the working. The data she added changed whether the answer applied to her café at all. And the corrected assumption changed the final price. In her notes she now keeps three lines for every pricing question, the costs, the target margin and the staffing assumption, so the next analysis starts from the right inputs.
```

Scene 10 — Pitfalls

```text
Three pitfalls. Using the heaviest model for everything and hitting your limits by lunchtime. Believing thinking means verified. Reasoning models still make mistakes, especially with missing or wrong inputs, so check key numbers. And trusting model names from old tutorials. Success looks like this. Your default handles most tasks without rework, and you switch to thinking deliberately, then check the assumptions.
```

Scene 11 — Usage and cost habits

```text
A word on usage. Thinking and Pro models use more of your plan's allowance, and on the API they cost more because they generate more tokens. So draft and iterate in Instant, then switch to Thinking for the final analysis. Batch related questions into one well structured request instead of ten small ones. And on team plans, agree which tasks justify the heaviest models, so routine work does not burn through everyone's limits.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Pick one simple task and one complex task from your real work this week. Run each in Instant and in Thinking, in fresh chats with the same prompt and files. For each of the four answers, score the quality from one to five and note how long it took and how much you would need to edit. You will probably find Instant is enough for the simple task and Thinking clearly wins on the complex one. Keep the table, and re run it whenever OpenAI changes the model picker.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me run the furniture store budget both ways. First I open the model picker and choose Instant. I paste the prompt from the lesson. Goal, a monthly ad budget split across Meta, TikTok and Google for a Lahore online furniture store in the fourth quarter. Data, six months of spend, revenue and cost per acquisition by channel. Constraints, a total budget, at least twenty percent on Google Search, a return on ad spend target of three. Output, a table, expected outcomes with assumptions, and a sensitivity check. The Instant answer arrives in seconds. It gives a sensible split, but no sensitivity check, and it quietly ignores the minimum Google share.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now I open a fresh chat, choose Thinking, and send exactly the same prompt with the same data. This time it takes longer, and the table respects every constraint, including the twenty percent Google minimum, and there is a sensitivity row for a twenty percent rise in cost per acquisition. I read the assumptions. Number three says costs stay flat in November, but November is our most competitive month. I correct it and ask for a rerun. The split shifts more budget to Google Search. In my model guide I record it, simple rewrites use Instant, budget planning uses Thinking.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Answers come from training plus context. Use Instant for everyday tasks, Thinking for complex ones, and the highest levels only when stakes justify it. Fix prompts before upgrading models, and learn the pattern rather than the names. Your next step: run one simple and one complex task in both modes, score them, and keep the table as your personal model guide.
```

### 2. Prompting fundamentals that work in ChatGPT

Lesson `prompting-fundamentals-in-chatgpt` · module “ChatGPT foundations” · target 9 min · 16 scenes · 1210 words · planned 8:47 (words ≈ 8:46) · status: **to produce**

| 1 | 0:00 | 22 | There are no magic words in ChatGPT. There is, however, a reliable structure that turns vague requests into usable first drafts. In this lecture you will learn the four part R G C F structure, how to separate pasted material, why examples beat adjectives, and how to iterate like an editor. | Prompting that works<br>• Role<br>• Goal<br>• Constraints<br>• Format | Layout: full-screen slide. Title card with four building blocks labelled R, G, C and F stacking into a solid tower. Timing (22s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-13s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~18s; hold to 22s. |
| 2 | 0:22 | 32 | Why bother with structure? Because whatever you leave out, the model fills in with an average guess. No audience means a generic audience. No length means whatever length feels typical. No tone means safe and bland. Imagine briefing a designer with just the words, make a poster. You would get a poster, but not yours. The four part structure is simply a checklist that makes sure your specific details replace the model's average ones. | Why structure matters<br>• The model fills gaps with averages<br>• Your details make it yours | Layout: slide. A prompt with three blank slots; each blank fills with a grey "average" placeholder; then the user fills them with specific details and the output card changes colour from grey to vivid. Timing (32s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-19s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~26s; hold to 32s. |
| 3 | 0:54 | 34 | R stands for role and context. Who you are, who the audience is, and the situation. G is the goal, one job stated plainly. C is constraints, meaning length, tone, spelling, things to avoid, facts that must not change and compliance rules. F is format, the shape of the output, a table with named columns, an email with a subject line, or bullet points. Current GPT models follow instructions closely, so be explicit. If a rule matters, say why. | R-G-C-F<br>• Role and context<br>• Goal<br>• Constraints<br>• Format | Layout: full-screen slide. The four blocks expand one at a time, each showing a short example sentence from a UK bakery scenario. Timing (34s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-20s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~27s; hold to 34s. |
| 4 | 1:28 | 19 | When you paste several things, a brief, brand rules and past posts, separate them with clear labelled headings or delimiters. It stops details bleeding from one document into another, and lets you refer to each precisely. Check every caption against the brand rules section. | Delimiters keep materials apart<br>• Brief<br>• Brand rules<br>• Past posts | Layout: full-screen slide. Text blocks separated by bold labelled headers slide apart so they no longer overlap. Timing (19s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-11s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~15s; hold to 19s. |
| 5 | 1:47 | 25 | Few shot prompting, which just means showing one or two examples of what you want, is the strongest steering tool you have. It beats a paragraph of adjectives like punchy and on brand. Say exactly what to copy, such as the structure, tone and length, and what not to copy, such as specific product names or prices. | Examples beat adjectives<br>• One or two samples<br>• Copy the structure and tone<br>• Not the facts | Layout: full-screen slide. Two example posts appear with arrows labelled structure and tone; a crossed-out label marks the product names as do not copy. Timing (25s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-15s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~20s; hold to 25s. |
| 6 | 2:12 | 19 | Add one line to important prompts. Ask me up to three questions before you start if anything important is missing. Now, instead of inventing your price or cut off time, ChatGPT asks. This single habit reduces invented details more than any clever wording. | Ask it to ask<br>• Up to three questions first | Layout: full-screen slide. Chat mock-up where ChatGPT responds with three short clarifying questions before drafting. Timing (19s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-11s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~15s; hold to 19s. |
| 7 | 2:31 | 31 | Then iterate precisely. If you left out context, edit your original message so the conversation branches from the better prompt. If the draft is close, give editor style feedback, keep the structure, cut to one hundred and twenty words, replace paragraph two with a customer example. Regenerate only when the prompt was good and you want a different alternative. And when a long chat drifts, start fresh with a short summary. | Iterate precisely<br>• Edit the original prompt<br>• Editor-style feedback<br>• Regenerate for alternatives<br>• New chat when drifting | Layout: four small panels. Timing (31s): heading in 0-2s; elements build 2-19s in step with narration; key element highlighted at ~25s; hold to 31s. |
| 8 | 3:02 | 23 | Ask for formats that save you time afterwards. A table you can paste straight into Google Sheets, with named columns. A CSV with specific headers for your email tool. <details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Prompting that works

```text
There are no magic words in ChatGPT. There is, however, a reliable structure that turns vague requests into usable first drafts. In this lecture you will learn the four part R G C F structure, how to separate pasted material, why examples beat adjectives, and how to iterate like an editor.
```

Scene 2 — Why structure matters

```text
Why bother with structure? Because whatever you leave out, the model fills in with an average guess. No audience means a generic audience. No length means whatever length feels typical. No tone means safe and bland. Imagine briefing a designer with just the words, make a poster. You would get a poster, but not yours. The four part structure is simply a checklist that makes sure your specific details replace the model's average ones.
```

Scene 3 — R-G-C-F

```text
R stands for role and context. Who you are, who the audience is, and the situation. G is the goal, one job stated plainly. C is constraints, meaning length, tone, spelling, things to avoid, facts that must not change and compliance rules. F is format, the shape of the output, a table with named columns, an email with a subject line, or bullet points. Current GPT models follow instructions closely, so be explicit. If a rule matters, say why.
```

Scene 4 — Delimiters keep materials apart

```text
When you paste several things, a brief, brand rules and past posts, separate them with clear labelled headings or delimiters. It stops details bleeding from one document into another, and lets you refer to each precisely. Check every caption against the brand rules section.
```

Scene 5 — Examples beat adjectives

```text
Few shot prompting, which just means showing one or two examples of what you want, is the strongest steering tool you have. It beats a paragraph of adjectives like punchy and on brand. Say exactly what to copy, such as the structure, tone and length, and what not to copy, such as specific product names or prices.
```

Scene 6 — Ask it to ask

```text
Add one line to important prompts. Ask me up to three questions before you start if anything important is missing. Now, instead of inventing your price or cut off time, ChatGPT asks. This single habit reduces invented details more than any clever wording.
```

Scene 7 — Iterate precisely

```text
Then iterate precisely. If you left out context, edit your original message so the conversation branches from the better prompt. If the draft is close, give editor style feedback, keep the structure, cut to one hundred and twenty words, replace paragraph two with a customer example. Regenerate only when the prompt was good and you want a different alternative. And when a long chat drifts, start fresh with a short summary.
```

Scene 8 — Formats for downstream tools

```text
Ask for formats that save you time afterwards. A table you can paste straight into Google Sheets, with named columns. A CSV with specific headers for your email tool. Markdown with H three headings for your website. A checklist with boxes. The less reformatting you do, the more time AI actually saves.
```

Scene 9 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. Write a product description for our dates, gives you something that could describe any dates on earth. Now the structured version. Role and context, I sell premium Medjool dates online in the UK, customers are gift buyers. Goal, a description for the product page. Constraints, sixty words, British spelling, no health claims. Format, one short paragraph then three bullet features. The second version is ready to publish with a light edit, because every choice that mattered came from you.
```

Scene 10 — Worked example: a Leeds bakery

```text
A small bakery in Leeds supplies forty cafés and wants to announce a gluten free range. The vague prompt produced a generic email. The structured version set the audience, café owners reading on their phones, the goal, add one item to next week's order, a length of one hundred and twenty to one hundred and fifty words, and one vital constraint, do not claim coeliac safe because the bakery is not certified. ChatGPT asked for product names and prices, then delivered a draft needing one small edit, and no claim the bakery could not back up. The bakery then reused the same prompt for a second announcement, a seasonal hot cross bun range, by changing only the goal and the product details. The first draft needed no edits at all, because the audience, tone, length and compliance rule were already baked into the template. That is the real payoff of structure. The second time is almost free.
```

Scene 11 — Big jobs in stages

```text
For anything longer than a page, work in stages you approve one at a time. First the outline, with three angles and no drafting yet. Then one section at a time. Then a critique from a named reader, without rewriting. Then targeted revisions, fix weaknesses one, two and four, keep everything else. Finally, package it, a subject line, a short summary and a table version. A mistake caught at the outline stage costs seconds. The same mistake caught in a finished draft costs an hour.
```

Scene 12 — Pitfalls

```text
Four pitfalls to avoid. Contradictory constraints, like comprehensive but under forty words. Giant prompts for multi stage jobs. Negative only instructions like do not be boring, instead of saying what you want. And pasting passwords, card data or confidential client details. Measure success by the share of first drafts you can use with light edits.
```

Scene 13 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Open a document called prompt library. Find the three prompts you use most often and rewrite each one with role, goal, constraints and format, plus one short example of what good output looks like, and the line, ask me up to three questions first. Run the old and new versions side by side and note how much editing each needs. Save the winners. Over the next month, every time a prompt works really well, add it. That library becomes one of your most valuable work assets.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me build the Leeds bakery email live. Role and context. I run a small artisan bakery in Leeds supplying forty cafés, the readers are café owners reading on their phones. Goal. Announce our new gluten free range and get them to add one item to next week's order. Constraints. One hundred and twenty to one hundred and fifty words, British spelling, friendly not salesy, do not claim coeliac safe because we are not certified, include the order cut off, Thursday at two. Format. A subject line under forty five characters, preview text, the body and a one line P S. Last line, ask me up to three questions first if anything important is missing.
```

Scene 15 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
ChatGPT asks for the product names and prices, and I answer in one line. The draft arrives at one hundred and forty two words. The subject line is thirty eight characters, and there is no coeliac claim, just made without gluten containing ingredients, which our baker confirms is accurate. I give editor feedback. Keep the structure, shorten the P S to one sentence. Then I ask for three subject line variants as a table I can paste into Google Sheets, with columns for subject, preview text and angle. Finally I save the full prompt to my prompt library under wholesale announcements.
```

Scene 16 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Use role, goal, constraints and format. Separate materials with delimiters, steer with examples, invite questions, and iterate precisely. Your next step is to start a prompt library, rewrite your three most used prompts with this structure, and compare how much editing the results need. You will reuse the winners in Projects and custom GPTs.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — The ChatGPT workspace

```text
ChatGPT has quietly become a whole workspace, with projects, custom GPTs, agents, coding tools, connected apps and a browser of its own. In the next few minutes you will get a map of where everything lives in September twenty twenty six, set it up securely, and complete a first real workflow.
```

Scene 2 — Why a tour matters

```text
Why start with a tour? Because most people use a tiny fraction of what ChatGPT can do. They type into the box and never discover Projects, canvas, deep research or connected apps. It is like owning a smartphone and only making calls. Knowing the map changes how you work, because when a task comes up, you know which tool fits. This lecture gives you that map, plus the settings that keep your work safe.
```

Scene 3 — Where ChatGPT runs

```text
ChatGPT runs on the web, in desktop apps for Mac and Windows, on iOS and Android, and inside OpenAI's own browser called ChatGPT Atlas. The core experience is the same everywhere, but mobile adds the camera and voice, and desktop is better for files and longer work.
```

Scene 4 — The map

```text
Here is the map. The composer is where you type, attach files and use voice, and its plus menu holds tools like web search, deep research, image creation, thinking, canvas and connected apps. Paid plans have a model picker. The sidebar holds your chats, Projects, GPTs, Codex, your images, and on eligible plans the agent experience for longer tasks called ChatGPT Work. Settings holds personalisation, apps, data controls and security.
```

Scene 5 — Plans in one breath

```text
Plans run from Free and a low cost plan called Go, through Plus and Pro for individuals, to Business, formerly called Team, and Enterprise and Edu for organisations. Higher plans add usage, more capable models, longer tasks and business controls. Prices and inclusions vary by country and change often, so always check OpenAI's pricing page.
```

Scene 6 — Setup: security and personalisation

```text
Now setup. Step one, security. Turn on multi factor authentication and check which devices are logged in. Step two, personalisation. Write custom instructions, pick a personality if you like, and look at what memory has saved about you. We go deeper on these in the next module.
```

Scene 7 — Setup: data and apps

```text
Step three, data controls. Decide whether your chats can be used to improve models, find Temporary Chat for sensitive one off questions, and review any shared links. Step four, apps. In Settings, open Apps and look at the default permission. Many apps now default to allow low risk actions, where ChatGPT acts without asking unless something is sensitive. Decide whether you would rather it always asked first.
```

Scene 8 — First workflow

```text
Step five, a first workflow. Attach a non confidential document and ask for a structured summary, purpose, five key points with references, decisions required, and anything unclear or missing. Then ask ChatGPT to turn it into a one page brief in canvas, make two targeted edits by highlighting text, and copy or export it. Step six, save the prompts that worked, or start a Project for that workstream.
```

Scene 9 — Mobile superpowers

```text
Do not forget mobile. Photograph a whiteboard, a receipt or a product label and ask about it. Use voice to think out loud on a walk or rehearse a pitch, then ask for a written summary. And use the share sheet to send articles or screenshots straight into ChatGPT.
```

Scene 10 — Simple example

```text
A simple example of the workspace at its best. You are at a restaurant in Dubai with an Arabic only menu. Open ChatGPT on your phone, take a photo, and ask it to translate the menu and suggest three vegetarian dishes under sixty dirhams. In a few seconds you have translated names, prices and suggestions. Camera, vision and reasoning working together, with no typing. That same combination works for receipts, product labels, whiteboards and handwritten notes at work.
```

Scene 11 — Worked example: a sales manager in Riyadh

```text
A new sales manager in Riyadh spends twenty five minutes on setup. Multi factor authentication on, custom instructions saying she sells B2B logistics software and prefers bullet summaries, memory reviewed, apps set to ask before acting. Her first workflow turns a thirty page RFP into a requirement list, which she moves into a Project for that deal. By Friday she has a Project for every active deal and a five prompt library. What made her week work was not any single feature. It was the order. Security first, so nothing leaked. Personalisation second, so every answer fitted her role. A Project third, so each deal had a home. And a prompt library last, so her best prompts were never lost. By the second week, preparing for a new RFP took her under an hour, by her own estimate, where it used to take most of a day.
```

Scene 12 — Where advanced features live

```text
Here is a signpost for the rest of the course. Keep a client's files and chats together in Projects. Give colleagues a reusable assistant with GPTs. Edit long documents in canvas. Get cited reports with deep research. Let ChatGPT work through multi step tasks with the Work agent. Connect Drive, Slack or your CRM through apps and plugins. Delegate coding to Codex. And build ChatGPT's models into your own software with the OpenAI API. Each has its own lesson.
```

Scene 13 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Block twenty five minutes today. Turn on multi factor authentication. Write your custom instructions. Look at your memory. Decide your data controls setting and find Temporary Chat. Open Settings, then Apps, and choose your default permission. Then run one real workflow, a structured summary of a non confidential document, turned into a one page brief in canvas with two targeted edits. When the timer ends, write down every setting you changed and why, so you can explain your choices to a colleague or manager.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me do the Riyadh sales manager's setup on screen. Settings, then security. I switch on multi factor authentication and scan the list of logged in devices. Then personalisation. In custom instructions I type, I sell B2B logistics software in Saudi Arabia and the UAE, I prefer bullet summaries with a one line answer first, flag anything I should verify. I leave personality on the default. Then I open memory, where there is an old entry about a previous job, and I delete it. Three screens, three minutes.
```

Scene 15 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Next, data controls. I decide the improve the model setting, and I find Temporary Chat so I know where it is. Then apps. I set my default permission to ask every time, because I will connect my work email later. Now the first workflow. I attach a thirty page RFP and ask for purpose, key requirements with page references, deadlines and anything unclear. I ask ChatGPT to turn the requirements into a checklist in canvas. I highlight one vague item and ask, split this into two separate requirements, and highlight another to add the page number. That checklist becomes the first file in a new Project for the deal.
```

Scene 16 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Know the map, secure your account, personalise deliberately, set your data and app permissions, and run one real workflow before you do anything clever. Your next step is the twenty five minute setup in the lesson. Do it today, and note every setting you changed and why.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Custom instructions and memory

```text
If you keep typing the same preferences into ChatGPT, British spelling, bullet points, no invented statistics, you are wasting time. Custom instructions and memory fix that. But badly managed memory can also surface stale or sensitive details at the worst moment. In this lecture you will learn to set both up well and keep them clean.
```

Scene 2 — Why personalisation matters

```text
Why take personalisation seriously? Because there are two quiet costs. The first is repetition. If you type the same preferences into every chat, you are wasting minutes every day. The second is stale memory. If ChatGPT remembers an old client or job title, those details can surface in the wrong place at the wrong time. Think of memory like a colleague's notebook about you. Helpful when it is accurate, awkward when it is out of date. So we set it up well and keep it tidy.
```

Scene 3 — Two kinds of personalisation

```text
There are two kinds of personalisation. Custom instructions, plus an optional personality, are what you tell ChatGPT to apply to every chat. Memory is what ChatGPT learns and keeps across chats, saved memories and, where enabled, reference to your past conversations. Both live in the personalisation settings. And Projects can have project only memory, which keeps what is learned in one Project from leaking into your other work.
```

Scene 4 — Good instructions

```text
Good custom instructions have two parts. About me, for example, a freelance brand strategist in Abu Dhabi working with hospitality and retail clients in the UAE and Saudi Arabia. And how to respond. British spelling, lead with a one line answer, tables for comparisons, flag assumptions, give two options for copy, and never invent statistics or sources. Keep it specific, stable, short and non confidential. Then test it with three typical questions.
```

Scene 5 — How memory works

```text
Memory works quietly. ChatGPT may save useful details, like prefers bullet summaries or works in hospitality. You can also tell it directly, remember that my agency is called Oasis Brand Studio. You can ask, what do you remember about me, and you can view, edit or delete memories in settings. Saved memories and chat history reference can be switched on or off separately, and business admins may control them.
```

Scene 6 — Temporary Chat

```text
Temporary Chat is your clean room. It does not use or update memory, and it is not saved to your history. Use it for a sensitive one off question, or to see what an unpersonalised answer looks like, which is surprisingly useful when you want an objective comparison.
```

Scene 7 — Monthly memory hygiene

```text
Once a month, spend five minutes on memory hygiene. Delete outdated details like a former client or old job title. Delete anything sensitive, like health, finances or other people's personal data. Correct wrong assumptions, for example, I am the marketing lead, not the owner. And keep client specific context in Projects, not in global memory, so it cannot spill into other clients' work.
```

Scene 8 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. In any chat, say, remember that I prefer tables for comparisons. ChatGPT saves it. Next week you ask, compare these two phone plans, and the answer arrives as a table without you asking. That is memory working for you. Now suppose you start preferring short bullet lists instead. Open memory in settings, delete the entry, and add a new custom instruction. You are always in control of what it remembers.
```

Scene 9 — Worked example: the exclusivity problem

```text
A beauty creator in Karachi signs a six month exclusivity deal with a skincare brand. But ChatGPT keeps suggesting collaborations with competitors, because memory holds details of past partnerships. She deletes those memory entries, adds a custom instruction saying until March, do not suggest skincare collaborations other than her partner brand, and tells ChatGPT to forget the old details. Her suggestions immediately respect her contract. Two weeks later, the brand's competitor launches a campaign and a follower asks her opinion. Because her instruction is explicit and dated, ChatGPT helps her write a neutral reply that does not mention a competing product. When March comes, she deletes the instruction and asks ChatGPT to forget the exclusivity, and her suggestions open up again. The rule lived exactly as long as the contract, which is the point.
```

Scene 10 — Pitfalls

```text
Four pitfalls. Stuffing instructions with one off task details. Putting client names, confidential data or credentials in instructions or memory. Forgetting memory exists and being caught out by stale details in client work. And assuming Temporary Chat changes your employer's or clients' data obligations. It controls history and memory, not contracts or law.
```

Scene 11 — What never goes in personalisation

```text
Some things never belong in instructions or memory. Passwords, API keys and card numbers, which belong in a password manager. Client names and deal terms, which belong in a client Project with project only memory. Sensitive health or family details you would not share at work. And other people's personal data. A quick test, if a colleague looked over your shoulder while ChatGPT recalled it, would you be comfortable? If not, delete it.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Rewrite your custom instructions with the about me and how to respond template, under one hundred and twenty words. Then ask ChatGPT, what do you remember about me? Read the answer carefully and delete anything outdated or sensitive in settings. Finally, ask the same question once in a normal chat and once in Temporary Chat, and compare. You will see exactly how much your personalisation is shaping the answers, and whether that is helping. Put a monthly reminder in your calendar to repeat the memory check.
```

Scene 13 — Instructions vs memory vs Projects

```text
Here is the key idea that ties this together. Custom instructions describe how you like answers, for every chat. Memory holds facts ChatGPT learns about you over time. And Projects hold the context for one client or initiative. When you are unsure where something belongs, ask yourself, should this apply to every conversation, should it be learned about me, or does it only matter for this one piece of work? Put it in that one place, and nowhere else.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me write the brand strategist's custom instructions. In the about me box, freelance brand strategist in Abu Dhabi, hospitality and retail clients in the UAE and Saudi Arabia, I write proposals, brand platforms and social strategies. In the how to respond box, British spelling, lead with a one line answer, tables for comparisons, flag assumptions, two options for copy, never invent statistics or sources, say data needed instead. I save and test with three questions, a tagline for a boutique hotel, a comparison of two loyalty programme models, and a market size question. The first two follow the rules. The third says data needed instead of inventing a number. Exactly right.
```

Scene 15 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Now memory. I ask, what do you remember about me? It lists my role, a former hotel client and my old day rate. I open memory settings and delete the former client and the old rate, since both could surface in the wrong proposal. Then a comparison. I ask the same question, draft a two line bio for a conference programme, in a normal chat and in Temporary Chat. The normal one uses my background and British spelling. The temporary one is generic. That side by side shows me exactly how much my personalisation is doing, and that it is helping.
```

Scene 16 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Custom instructions say how you want answers. Memory learns about you and needs regular review. Temporary Chat gives you a clean, unsaved room. And client context belongs in Projects. Your next step: rewrite your custom instructions using the template, ask ChatGPT what it remembers, clean it up, and set a monthly reminder.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Projects in ChatGPT

```text
If you work on the same client, product or report week after week, you should not be re explaining it every time. ChatGPT Projects give each workstream its own home with shared files, instructions and memory. In this lecture you will learn how to set one up properly, when to use a Project instead of a custom GPT, and how to keep it accurate.
```

Scene 2 — Why Projects matter

```text
Why do Projects matter? Because without them, your work on a client or initiative is scattered across dozens of unrelated chats, and every new chat starts from zero. A Project is one home for that work, with the files, the rules and the history in one place. And with project only memory, what ChatGPT learns about that client stays with that client. Think of it as a labelled filing drawer instead of papers spread across your desk.
```

Scene 3 — What a Project is

```text
A Project groups related chats with shared files and project instructions. Projects are available on free and paid plans, with limits that vary. Each Project can use default memory or project only memory. With project only memory, what ChatGPT learns inside the Project stays there, and chats can reference each other without dragging in unrelated conversations. On business plans you can share Projects with colleagues.
```

Scene 4 — Project, GPT or chat?

```text
Choose the right container. A one off question belongs in a normal chat. Your ongoing work on a client or initiative, with evolving files and chats, belongs in a Project. A reusable assistant with fixed instructions that colleagues can open and use is a custom GPT, covered in module five. And repeatable procedures or bundled workflows belong in plugins and skills, which we cover in module six.
```

Scene 5 — Setup step 1 and 2

```text
Setting up well takes five steps. One Project per client or initiative, because mixing clients mixes rules and facts. Upload current, clearly named files, like twenty twenty six September price list approved, and remove superseded versions. If both old and new price lists sit in the Project, ChatGPT has to choose between conflicting sources, and that is where wrong prices come from.
```

Scene 6 — Setup step 3

```text
Step three, focused instructions. For a premium dates brand selling in the UAE and UK, that might be: use product names and prices exactly as in the approved product sheet, British English for the UK, English and Arabic for the UAE, disclosure labels on paid partnerships, never invent prices, reviews, awards or statistics, write data needed instead, and end every deliverable with a checks needed list.
```

Scene 7 — Setup steps 4 and 5

```text
Step four, choose project only memory for client work. Step five, test with a trap question the files cannot answer, such as the price of a product that is not listed. A good Project says it is not in the files. If it invents a number, tighten the instructions and check for conflicting documents.
```

Scene 8 — Working inside a Project

```text
Inside the Project, keep one chat per deliverable. Ask ChatGPT to use earlier Project chats, for example, using the decisions from the kickoff chat, draft the brief. Save important approved outputs back into the Project as files so future chats use them. And refresh files monthly, because Project knowledge is a snapshot, not a live database.
```

Scene 9 — Simple example

```text
Here is a simple, personal example. You are renovating a kitchen. Create a Project and upload three builders' quotes, your measurements and a budget sheet. In one chat, ask ChatGPT to compare the quotes line by line and flag anything missing, like one quote leaving out waste removal. In another chat, ask for a task timeline based on the chosen quote. Both chats draw on the same files, and neither needs you to re upload anything. The business version works exactly the same way, just with clients instead of cupboards.
```

Scene 10 — Worked example: a Jeddah fragrance launch

```text
A marketing lead at a fragrance brand in Jeddah creates a Project called winter launch. Files include the approved product sheet, the brand voice guide, a dated summary of the regulator's disclosure guidance, and last year's results. Instructions set bilingual rules and banned claims. Chats cover messaging, the influencer brief, the email sequence and the FAQ. When pricing changes mid campaign, she replaces the product sheet and notes the change date in the instructions, and every later chat uses the new price. Midway through the campaign, a new team member joins. Instead of a long handover call, the lead shares the Project. The newcomer reads the instructions, sees the current product sheet and the decisions in the kickoff chat, and drafts her first Eid post that afternoon with the right prices and the right disclosure label. The Project became the handover document without anyone writing one.
```

Scene 11 — Monthly maintenance

```text
Give every important Project fifteen minutes a month. Remove superseded files and add the newest approved ones with dated names. Add one instruction for each recurring error you logged, and delete rules you no longer need. Archive finished chats and rename active ones clearly. Re run the trap question. And on shared Projects, confirm that only the right people have access. Agree a naming convention too, such as client, workstream and year, so anyone can find the right Project in seconds.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Create a Project for one real workstream you return to every week. Paste in focused instructions based on the template, with exact names, disclosure rules and a never invent rule. Upload three current, clearly named files, and choose project only memory if it is client work. Run two real tasks. Then ask a trap question the files cannot answer. If ChatGPT says it is not in the files, you are ready. If it invents something, fix the instructions and look for conflicting files.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me set up the dates brand Project. I create a new Project and name it using our convention, Al Waha Dates, social, twenty twenty six. I paste the instructions. Use product names and prices exactly as in the approved product sheet, British English for the UK, English and Arabic for the UAE, disclosure labels on paid partnerships, never invent prices, reviews or awards, write data needed, and end with a checks needed list. I upload three files with dated names, the product sheet, the disclosure rules and the brand voice guide. Then I choose project only memory, because this is client work.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
First task, in a chat called Eid campaign captions, five bilingual captions for the premium gift box. The prices match the sheet, and the paid partnership label is included. Now the trap. What is the price of the two kilogram family box? That product is not listed, and ChatGPT says it is not in the project files and writes data needed. Next, a second chat. Using the decisions from the kickoff chat, draft the influencer brief. It pulls the budget range and the three content pillars we agreed earlier, without me pasting anything. The Project is working as a single home for this client.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Use a Project for every recurring workstream, with clean, current files and focused instructions. Choose project only memory for client work, run a trap question before you trust it, and refresh monthly. On business plans, share carefully and name an owner. Your next step: set up one Project for real recurring work, run two tasks and a trap question, and refine the instructions.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Data analysis you can trust

```text
Uploading a spreadsheet to ChatGPT and asking what does this tell me feels like magic. It is also one of the easiest ways to present a confident, wrong number to your boss. In this lecture you will learn a six step workflow that turns ChatGPT into a genuinely reliable analyst, and the questions that expose weak conclusions before anyone else does.
```

Scene 2 — Why data needs a method

```text
Why do we need a method for data? Because a chart always looks authoritative, even when the data behind it is a mess. Duplicate rows, text stored as numbers, a metric defined three different ways. Those problems do not announce themselves. They quietly change the answer. Think of it like cooking. The dish can look beautiful and still be made with the wrong ingredient. The six step method makes sure you check the ingredients before you serve the result.
```

Scene 3 — Why code matters

```text
ChatGPT handles two kinds of files differently. Documents it reads and reasons over. Data files, like spreadsheets and CSVs, it can analyse by writing and running Python code in a secure sandbox. That is the key. Numbers computed with code are far more reliable than numbers estimated in a paragraph. You can also pull files from connected apps like Google Drive or SharePoint, where your plan and admin allow.
```

Scene 4 — Step 1: describe first

```text
Step one, describe the data before analysing it. Ask for the number of rows, each column with its type and an example, missing values, duplicate rows and anything that looks like an outlier or data entry error. And say, do not analyse yet. This single step catches most of the problems that ruin an analysis.
```

Scene 5 — Steps 2 and 3

```text
Step two, clean deliberately. You decide the rules. Remove exact duplicates. Treat blank regions as unknown. Exclude test orders. And ask how many rows each rule affected. Step three, define your metrics. Engagement rate equals likes, comments, shares and saves, divided by impressions. Revenue is gross sales minus refunds, excluding VAT. Ambiguous metrics produce confident, wrong comparisons.
```

Scene 6 — Steps 4 and 5

```text
Step four, analyse with a specific question. Which content format had the highest median engagement rate in the third quarter, by platform? Show the table and the code. Median is often more honest than the average when a few viral posts skew everything. Step five, visualise simply. Sorted bars, labelled axes, no three D effects, and ask for an exportable file when you need to share it.
```

Scene 7 — Step 6: verify

```text
Step six, verify. Spot check two numbers yourself in Excel or Sheets. Ask to see the rows behind the top result. And always ask how many items are in each group. A chart that says Stories performed best looks very different once you learn it is based on three posts.
```

Scene 8 — Read results critically

```text
Read every result critically. Small samples are anecdotes, not trends. Tuesday posts may perform better because of what you posted on Tuesdays, not the day itself. Analysing only completed campaigns hides the ones cancelled early. And mixed currency columns, dirhams, riyals and pounds, must be converted with a stated rate and date. A great habit is to ask, what are the three biggest reasons this conclusion might be wrong?
```

Scene 9 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. Upload a small sales file with fifty rows and ask, before any analysis, to describe the data. ChatGPT reports two duplicate rows and one blank region. You tell it to remove the duplicates and label the blank as unknown. Then ask for total sales by month, with the code. Pick one month and add it up yourself in a spreadsheet. It matches. You now trust the result, and you know exactly why. That is the whole method in miniature.
```

Scene 10 — Worked example: a UAE brand’s social review

```text
A social media manager in the UAE exports six months of Instagram and TikTok analytics. ChatGPT finds fourteen duplicate rows and a column where some impressions were stored as text. She sets cleaning rules, defines engagement rate and asks for the median by format and platform. Carousels beat reels on Instagram for her account. She checks the counts, twenty two carousels and forty one reels, spot checks two values in Sheets, and presents it as a pattern worth testing, with a four week experiment plan, not as a law. Four weeks later, she reruns exactly the same cleaned workflow on the new export. Because the cleaning rules and the metric definition were saved as a recipe, the comparison is like for like. The carousel lead holds on Instagram but shrinks, and on TikTok nothing changes. Her report says so plainly, with the post counts beside each figure, and her manager approves a small shift in the content mix rather than a dramatic one.
```

Scene 11 — Privacy first

```text
Before uploading, remove names, emails, phone numbers and order numbers you do not need. Use your organisation's approved workspace for company data, not a personal account. And delete working files from chats when you are finished, where appropriate. If a colleague later asks for the source data, you will be glad you kept only what the analysis needed.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Export a real dataset that contains no personal data, your own social analytics, website traffic by page, or a public dataset. Upload it and work through all six steps, describe, clean, define metrics, analyse against one clear question, visualise simply, and verify. Spot check two numbers yourself in a spreadsheet, and ask how many rows sit behind the top result. Save the prompts as a data analysis recipe in your prompt library or a Project, so next month's analysis takes half the time.
```

Scene 13 — Common mistakes

```text
Let's name the common mistakes, because you will see them in other people's reports. Asking for conclusions before checking data quality. Using the average when one viral post or one huge order distorts everything, so ask for the median too. Mixing currencies without a stated conversion rate and date. And presenting a result based on a handful of rows as if it were a trend. If you avoid those four, your analysis will already be better than most.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me run the UAE social review. I upload the six month export and paste the describe first prompt. Rows, columns with types and examples, missing values, duplicates and outliers, and do not analyse yet. ChatGPT runs code and reports fourteen duplicate rows and an impressions column where some values are stored as text with commas. I set the cleaning rules. Remove exact duplicates, convert impressions to numbers, and exclude two test posts published from our staging account. I ask how many rows each rule affected, and it reports fourteen, thirty one and two. Now I trust the table I am about to analyse.
```

Scene 15 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
Next, the metric. Engagement rate equals likes, comments, shares and saves, divided by impressions. Then the question. Median engagement rate by format and platform, with the number of posts in each group, and show the code. On Instagram, carousels lead, from twenty two posts, against reels from forty one. I ask for a simple sorted bar chart. Then my check. I open the cleaned file in Google Sheets, filter Instagram carousels, and calculate the median myself. It matches. I check one reel figure too. Both match, so the finding goes into my report, framed as a pattern to test over four weeks.
```

Scene 16 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Describe the data first, clean deliberately, define metrics, analyse against a clear question, visualise simply and verify. Question every conclusion and protect personal data. Your next step: run the six steps on a real, non sensitive dataset, spot check two numbers, and save the prompts as your reusable data analysis recipe.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Images in ChatGPT

```text
ChatGPT can look at images and it can make them. Used well, that means faster concept work, sharper creative reviews and fewer design bottlenecks. Used carelessly, it means misleading ads and embarrassing typos. In this lecture you will learn both capabilities, a prompt formula that works, what changed in September twenty twenty six, and the rules that keep you honest.
```

Scene 2 — Why images need a method

```text
Why do images need a method? Because a beautiful image is not the same as an accurate one. AI can produce a gorgeous product shot where the label text is garbled or the product shape is subtly wrong. For concept work that is fine. For an ad, it can mislead customers and break advertising rules. And on the vision side, a quick glance at a screenshot can misread a number. The methods in this lecture keep your images both beautiful and honest.
```

Scene 3 — Two capabilities

```text
Vision means ChatGPT analyses images you give it, screenshots, photos, charts, handwriting and design drafts. Image generation means it creates new images from descriptions and edits existing ones, powered by OpenAI's GPT Image models. In September twenty twenty six OpenAI released ChatGPT Images two point five, which added Sketch, so you can turn your own rough drawing into a finished image, and made generation faster. The old standalone DALL E GPT was retired at the end of August, and image creation now lives directly in ChatGPT.
```

Scene 4 — Vision: describe first

```text
For vision, use the describe first pattern. Ask ChatGPT to describe everything it sees, text, numbers, labels, colours and layout, without interpreting. Then answer your question using only that description. Then list anything it was unsure about reading. This separates seeing from thinking, so a misread number is caught before it becomes a conclusion. Crop to what matters, and label multiple images clearly.
```

Scene 5 — The image prompt formula

```text
For generation, use a formula. Subject, a ceramic coffee cup with Arabic calligraphy. Setting, a marble café counter in Old Dubai at golden hour. Style, editorial product photography. Composition, the cup on the left third with clean space at the top for a headline. Lighting and mood, warm and inviting. Format, four by five vertical for the Instagram feed. And if you need text in the image, put the exact words in quotes and check the spelling in the result.
```

Scene 6 — Iterate one change at a time

```text
Then iterate one change at a time. Same image, but cooler morning light. Same image, cup moved right. Keep a prompt log so you can reproduce what worked. And if you already know the composition you want, sketch rough shapes and let Sketch render them. It is often faster than describing a layout in words.
```

Scene 7 — Editing images

```text
You can also edit. Upload an image and ask to change the background or remove an object, or select part of a generated image and describe the change. Always compare the edit with the original, because faces, logos and product details can change in ways you did not ask for.
```

Scene 8 — Responsible use

```text
Now the rules. Do not misrepresent real products. Use AI for concepts, mood boards and backgrounds, and real photos of the actual product in ads, or composite real shots into AI backgrounds. Do not create images that impersonate real people or imply endorsements. OpenAI adds C two P A provenance metadata to generated images, and social platforms have their own AI labelling rules, so follow them and your advertising codes. Avoid copying protected characters, logos or a living artist's distinctive style. And check the details, hands, text, logos and reflections.
```

Scene 9 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. You need a header image for a blog post about working from cafés. Using the formula, you ask for a laptop and a cup of coffee on a wooden café table, soft morning light, editorial photography style, subject on the right third with space on the left for a headline, wide sixteen by nine format. The first version is close but a little cold. You change one thing, warmer golden hour light, and log the change. Two iterations, and you have a header you can use.
```

Scene 10 — Worked example: a Karachi restaurant

```text
A restaurant in Karachi is launching a new biryani platter. The team uses ChatGPT to generate three mood board images for the menu design and social teaser, clearly as concept art, and uses Sketch to test a flat lay layout. For the launch ads, they photograph the real dish in the layout ChatGPT helped design, then use vision to check the ad drafts for text legibility on mobile. No customer ever sees an AI image presented as the real food. The team also used vision on the draft ads. ChatGPT flagged that the price on the mobile story ad sat too close to the edge and would be cut off by the platform's interface, and that the Urdu headline was too small at thumbnail size. Both were fixed before launch. Concept images helped them design faster, and vision helped them publish cleaner, but every customer facing image showed the real dish.
```

Scene 11 — A creative-review prompt

```text
Here is a vision workflow worth saving. Ask ChatGPT to review your ad creatives one by one. Describe the layout, headline, call to action, colours and small print. Score offer clarity at thumbnail size, brand fit, hierarchy and mobile legibility. Flag any claim that needs evidence and any missing ad disclosure label. Suggest one concrete fix per creative, and end with a ranking table. Then treat the scores as hypotheses, and let real audience tests decide.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Create one concept image for your brand or a current project using the seven part formula. Iterate three times, changing only one element each time, and log each change. Notice which change made the biggest difference. Then take a screenshot of one of your ads or landing pages, crop out any personal data, and run the describe first review. Did the description catch anything you had not noticed, like small text that becomes unreadable on mobile? Save both prompts for next time.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me create the café concept image. I type the prompt using the formula. Subject, a ceramic coffee cup with Arabic calligraphy. Setting, a marble counter in an Old Dubai café at golden hour. Style, editorial product photography. Composition, cup on the left third, clean space at the top for a headline. Lighting, warm with soft shadows. Format, four by five vertical. And the text, the words Morning Ritual in quotes. The first render is lovely, but when I zoom in, the headline reads Morning Ritaul. Text in images always needs checking.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
I iterate one change at a time. Same image, fix the headline spelling to Morning Ritual. Version two is correct. Same image, cooler morning light. Version three is fresher, and the team prefers it. Then I try Sketch. I draw a rough flat lay, a cup in the centre, a date bowl top right and a notebook bottom left, and upload it. The result follows my layout closely. I log every version in a small table, the version, the one change and the result, so anyone can reproduce the approved look. And because this is concept art for a real café, the final ad will use a real photo shot in this layout.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Vision analyses, generation creates. Describe before you conclude. Use the seven part prompt formula, iterate one change at a time, and use Sketch for layouts. And never let AI images misrepresent real products or people. Your next step: create one concept image for your brand, refine it three times while logging each change, and run a describe first review on one of your ads.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Voice

```text
Some of your best thinking happens away from a keyboard, on a walk, in the car, pacing before a big meeting. ChatGPT's voice mode turns those moments into rehearsal and planning time. In this lecture you will learn what voice is best for, how to set up a role play that genuinely prepares you, and the privacy etiquette that matters.
```

Scene 2 — Why voice matters

```text
Why use voice at all? Because a lot of good thinking happens away from a keyboard, walking, commuting, pacing before a meeting. Voice lets you capture and develop that thinking. And for conversations that matter, rehearsal beats improvisation every time. Athletes practise the difficult moves, not the easy ones. Voice mode lets you practise the difficult conversation, the price objection, the upset client, before it happens for real.
```

Scene 3 — What voice is good for

```text
Voice lets you talk naturally and hear spoken replies on mobile, desktop and web, and you can switch between voice and text in the same conversation. It is ideal for thinking out loud, rehearsing sales calls and difficult conversations, practising a language, dictating rough ideas, and any moment when your hands are busy. It can also use tools like search and your connected apps, under the same permissions as text.
```

Scene 4 — Set the frame first

```text
Voice conversations drift fast without a frame, so open with a short setup. Here is one. You are a sceptical procurement manager at a Dubai logistics company. I am selling route optimisation software. Push back on price, integration effort and data security. Keep replies under thirty seconds. After ten minutes, stop and give me feedback, my three strongest moments, my three weakest answers, and a better phrasing for the weakest.
```

Scene 5 — Make it realistically hard

```text
Easy role plays build false confidence. Ask for specific objections from your real market, like your competitor quoted twenty percent less. Ask for interruptions and follow up questions. Set a difficulty, such as hard mode, impatient and distracted. And ask for feedback against criteria, clarity, empathy, objection handling, and whether you secured a next step.
```

Scene 6 — Always end in writing

```text
Voice is ephemeral, so finish every session in writing. Ask for a written summary with key points, decisions, action items with owners, and anything you promised to follow up on. Then paste it into your notes, your CRM or the relevant Project. Without this step, most of the value evaporates.
```

Scene 7 — Language practice

```text
For languages, state your level and goal, for example, I am an intermediate Arabic learner preparing for client meetings in Riyadh. Ask for gentle corrections after each reply, or a summary of mistakes at the end. Request a vocabulary list from the conversation. And practise real scenarios, introductions, small talk, ordering and price negotiation.
```

Scene 8 — Consent and privacy

```text
Now etiquette. Do not secretly capture other people's voices or conversations, because recording laws differ by country and many require consent. Avoid client names, deal values and personal data when speaking in public places. Never read out card numbers, passwords or ID numbers. And for real meetings, use approved transcription tools and tell participants, in line with your policy and local law.
```

Scene 9 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. You are running a stall at a weekend market. On a three minute walk, start voice mode and say, help me plan my stall for Saturday, ask me one question at a time. It asks what you are selling, how you take payment and what the weather might do. At the end, ask for a written checklist. You arrive home with float cash, card reader, price labels and a rain cover on a list you did not have to type.
```

Scene 10 — Worked example: pitching a UK distributor

```text
A founder in Lahore is preparing to pitch a UK distributor. She runs three ten minute voice role plays, a friendly buyer, a sceptical finance director and an impatient category manager. After each, she asks for feedback and a better phrasing for her weakest answer. By round three she handles the objection, your shipping times are too slow, with a specific plan instead of an apology. She saves the summaries to her deal Project and turns the best phrasings into a one page talk track. In the real meeting, the distributor's buyer raised exactly the shipping objection, in almost the same words. She answered with the plan she had rehearsed, a UK stock buffer and a two week lead time for replenishment, and the conversation moved on to volumes. Afterwards she added the buyer's real questions to her deal Project, so her next rehearsal will be even closer to reality.
```

Scene 11 — Five ready-made sessions

```text
To make voice a habit, keep five opening lines saved on your phone. Sales rehearsal, play a sceptical buyer and give me feedback after ten minutes. Interview prep, ask one behavioural question at a time and score my answers. Difficult conversation, play an upset client whose campaign underperformed and do not make it easy. Idea capture, ask clarifying questions and write an outline at the end. And language practice at your level, with gentle corrections and a vocabulary list. With these saved, you can start a useful session in seconds, wherever you are.
```

Scene 12 — Pitfalls

```text
Four pitfalls. Starting without a role or goal, so the conversation wanders. Practising in easy mode, which never tests you. Forgetting the written summary, so insights disappear. And speaking sensitive details in public, or capturing others without consent. You will know voice is working when real conversations feel familiar, because you have already heard the hardest objections.
```

Scene 13 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Think of a real conversation coming up this month, a sales call, a salary discussion, a difficult client update. Start voice mode with the setup template, a specific role, your goal, short replies, hard mode, and feedback after ten minutes. Run the role play. Ask for a better phrasing for your weakest answer and practise it once more out loud. Then ask for a written summary with your best lines and save it where you will see it before the real conversation.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me rehearse the Dubai logistics pitch. I start voice mode and speak the setup. You are a sceptical procurement manager at a Dubai logistics company. I am selling route optimisation software. Push back on price, integration effort and data security, interrupt me sometimes, keep replies under thirty seconds, and after ten minutes stop and give feedback. The buyer opens with, your competitor quoted us twenty percent less. I answer with our fuel savings case. Before I finish, it interrupts, and how many months will my IT team lose integrating this? It is uncomfortable, which is exactly the point.
```

Scene 15 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
After ten minutes, the feedback. Strongest, the fuel savings numbers. Weakest, my integration answer, which was vague and apologetic. It suggests a better phrasing. Most clients are live in six weeks, with our team doing the connector work, and we can start with one depot. I say it out loud twice until it feels natural. Then I ask for a written summary with my best lines, the objections raised and the follow up questions I should prepare for. I paste it into the deal Project, so I can read it again in the car before the real meeting.
```

Scene 16 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Frame every voice session with a role, goal, reply length and feedback moment. Make practice realistically hard. Always end with a written summary. And respect consent and privacy. Your next step: run a ten minute role play for a real conversation coming up this month, ask for feedback on your weakest answer, and save the written summary.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Narration per scene (paste into ElevenLabs)</summary>

Scene 1 — Web search in ChatGPT

```text
ChatGPT's training has a cut off, but your clients ask about this week. Web search closes that gap, if you use it well. In this lecture you will learn when to search and when not to, how to write search prompts that return authoritative sources, how to read those sources critically, and how to keep private information out of your queries.
```

Scene 2 — Why search needs judgement

```text
Why does search need judgement? Because current is not the same as correct. Search brings in whatever the web says today, and the web contains official pages, careful journalism, outdated blogs and affiliate lists written to sell. A citation tells you where a claim came from, not whether it is true. Think of search results like witnesses. Some are experts, some are guessing, and some have something to sell. Your job is to know which is which.
```

Scene 3 — When to search

```text
ChatGPT can search the web during a conversation and show sources as inline citations. It may search automatically, or you can pick search from the plus menu. Use it for current facts like prices, news and policy changes, local information about a country or city, and any claim you will repeat to a client or publish. Skip it for brainstorming, rewriting, explaining stable concepts or working on your own documents, because it just adds time and noise.
```

Scene 4 — A strong search prompt

```text
Specify the question, the region, the time frame and the sources you trust. For example, what are the current rules for disclosing paid influencer content on Instagram for audiences in the UAE, prioritising official government, regulator and platform policy pages, with the date of each source, a citation after each claim, and a final note on anything unclear or recently changed. Compare that with influencer rules UAE, question mark, which invites a vague answer from whatever ranks well.
```

Scene 5 — Read sources critically

```text
For every important claim, open the source and confirm it says what the answer claims, because paraphrases drift. Check the date. Check authority, so a regulator beats a blog. If sources disagree, prefer the primary source and note the discrepancy. And ask ChatGPT which parts are directly stated in sources and which are its own inferences.
```

Scene 6 — Useful follow-ups

```text
Follow ups make search far more useful. Find the primary source for that figure. Search again using only official sources. What changed in the last twelve months? And my favourite, give me the sources as a table with title, publisher, date, URL and what each one supports. That table drops straight into your research notes.
```

Scene 7 — Beware manipulation

```text
The web contains misleading content, SEO spam and even hidden instructions written for AI systems, which is called prompt injection. Treat search results as data, not instructions. Be especially sceptical of recommendations, like the best tool is, that trace back to a vendor's own page or an affiliate list.
```

Scene 8 — Privacy in queries

```text
Search queries can leave your conversation to reach search providers. So keep personal data and confidential details out of them. Research the general question, what is the current VAT registration threshold in the UK, rather than the private situation, is Client X over the threshold with ninety three thousand pounds turnover. Then apply the answer to your private details inside your approved workspace.
```

Scene 9 — Simple example

```text
A simple example. Ask ChatGPT with search, what are the Dubai Mall opening hours this Friday? It might cite the mall's official site and a travel blog. If they disagree, and they often do around holidays, open the official page and use that. It takes ten seconds, and it is exactly the habit you will need for more important questions, like regulations and prices, where the stakes are much higher than a wasted trip.
```

Scene 10 — Worked example: a competitor’s claim

```text
An account manager in London hears that a competitor now offers same day delivery across the UAE. She asks ChatGPT to search official sources for the competitor's delivery terms, with dates. The answer cites the competitor's own delivery page, same day in Dubai and Abu Dhabi only, with a cut off time, and a news article making the broader claim. She opens both, confirms the narrower official terms, and briefs her client accurately, noting the date she checked. She also set a reminder in her verification log to recheck the competitor's delivery page in three months, because delivery promises change often. When she checked again, the competitor had added Sharjah. Her client briefing was updated in two minutes, with the new date, because the log showed exactly which slide and which claim depended on that page.
```

Scene 11 — Keep a verification log

```text
Keep a simple verification log for facts that matter. The claim, the publisher, the source date, the date you checked, whether it held up, and notes. Ask ChatGPT to output its sources in that format, and filling the log takes seconds. When a rule or price changes next quarter, the log tells you exactly which decks, posts or proposals need updating, which is the difference between a professional and someone who just pasted an answer.
```

Scene 12 — Try this now

```text
Try this now. Pick one fact you rely on at work that could have changed, a platform's ad policy, a fee, a regulatory threshold, a competitor's offer. Write a search prompt that names the region, the time frame and the source types you trust, and ask for dates and a citation after each claim. Open at least two sources and check the wording. Record the result in a verification log. If the fact has changed, you just saved yourself from repeating something out of date.
```

Scene 13 — Watch me do it, part 1

```text
Let me check the competitor claim from the lesson. I switch on search and type, what are the current same day delivery terms of the competitor across the UAE? Prioritise the competitor's official delivery page and reputable news, give the date of each source, cite after each claim, and finish with anything unclear. The answer streams in with inline citations. I open the sources panel. One citation is the competitor's own delivery terms page, one is a regional news article from four months ago. Already I can see a difference, the article says across the UAE, the official page lists specific cities.
```

Scene 14 — Watch me do it, part 2

```text
I open both. The official page says same day delivery in Dubai and Abu Dhabi for orders placed by noon. The article makes the broader claim. So the primary source wins. I ask a follow up. Give me the sources as a table with title, publisher, date, URL and what each supports. I paste that into my verification log and mark the claim partly verified, same day only in two emirates, with today's date. My client briefing now says exactly that, and if the competitor expands later, the log tells me which slide to update.
```

Scene 15 — Recap and next step

```text
Recap. Search when facts are current, local or will be repeated. Write prompts with region, time frame and source preferences. Open, date check and triangulate the sources that matter, and keep private details out of queries. For multi source investigations, use deep research, which is next. Your next step: re check one fact you rely on at work and log the claim, source, date and whether it held up.
```

</details>
